Two translations were necessary and are stated rather than silently applied:

1. **Day-grain → month-grain.** None of the three source marts carries day-level ingestion or utilization events — every one is account-per-**month** (`fact_workflow_chain_events`, `fact_committed_vs_utilized_monthly` are both monthly; POC resolution is a single close-dated event, not a daily series). "14+ days" and "30 days" become "2+ consecutive months" and "the first full calendar month post-close" respectively — the same day-to-month translation Activation's trailing-baseline treatment and the account-health score's "N consecutive months" churn language already establish elsewhere in this project.
2. **The literal 60%/50% numbers do not survive contact with this data's real distribution**, and keeping them unchanged would have produced rules that fire almost constantly rather than genuine outliers (see the grounding below for each). Both are lowered to sit at a real, non-trivial minority of the observed distribution instead — the same discipline the data-quality-governance entry's PROPOSED floors and the segment-migration entry's 10% firmographic-rescore floor both apply: a threshold that fires on the overwhelming majority of cases is not a trigger, it is a constant.

### Rule 1 — Ingestion-without-completion (`ingestion_without_completion`)

**Rule**: `full_chain_completion_rate < 0.70` sustained for **2+ consecutive months** — fires once, at the month the streak first reaches that length (not once per month the episode continues). Source: `fact_workflow_chain_events` (`account_id`, `month`, `full_chain_completion_rate` = `downstream_actions / upstream_actions`). Action: *"Escalate to CS for workflow-chain diagnostic outreach."*

**Grounding (PROPOSED, not yet confirmed)**: `full_chain_completion_rate` across all 142,942 account-months is genuinely **bimodal with an empty gap** — a histogram in 0.021-wide bins shows roughly 470–530 account-months per bin from 0.349 up to 0.644, then **zero account-months in every bin from 0.644 to 0.834**, then 5,000–22,000 per bin from 0.834 to 0.981. **0.70 sits in the middle of that empty gap** — there is no distributional knife-edge near it in either direction, since nothing in the real data lies between 0.644 and 0.834 at all. This is a real, designed subpopulation, not sampling noise: **every observed below-0.70 episode is 3–5 consecutive months long** (1,461 episodes, minimum run length 3, none of length 1 or 2) — a **2-consecutive-month** persistence gate is chosen deliberately below that observed minimum, so it is guaranteed to catch every real episode (since none is shorter than 3 months) while still being unable to fire on an isolated single bad month, because no such case exists anywhere in this data to begin with.

### Rule 2 — POC pass rate below threshold (`poc_pass_rate_below_threshold`)

**Rule**: fires for a specific Enterprise opportunity when **its own POC resolves `fail` AND the trailing 90-day cohort POC pass rate** (every other Enterprise POC that closed strictly before this one, within the trailing window, cohort size ≥ 5) **sits below 30%**. Source: `fact_opportunities` (`account_id`, `opportunity_id`, `close_date`, `poc_outcome`, `segment = 'Enterprise'`). Action: *"Escalate to Sales Engineering leadership for POC process review."*

**Grounding (PROPOSED, not yet confirmed)**: overall Enterprise POC pass rate is **42.5%** (192 of 452, at `as_of_date` 2025-12-31) — the build spec's illustrative 60% floor sits *above* the population mean, and a trailing-90-day rolling pass rate computed per-POC (n=424 resolved rows with a full window) has **median 40.0%, IQR [34.9%, 48.1%]**; a 60% floor would fire on **99.8%** of resolved POCs — not a trigger, a constant. **30% sits close to the 10th percentile (28.7%)** of that same trailing-rate distribution — comfortably below the median so it does not fire near-continuously, while still catching a real, non-trivial minority (12.97% of trailing-rate observations sit below 0.30) of genuinely underperforming windows.

**A structural limitation, found at build time, not glossed over**: `fact_opportunities.account_id` is **NULL on every lost opportunity** — the identical gap `analytics/marketing_attribution.py`'s "Measure caveat" already documents for lost new-business deals, confirmed here to hold for lost Enterprise/POC-stage opportunities generally. Of the 452 Enterprise POCs at 2025-12-31, only the 113 that closed **Won** carry a traceable `account_id` (91 pass, 22 fail-but-still-won); the other 339 (101 pass-but-lost, 238 fail-and-lost) have no account this operational log's `(rule_id, account, timestamp, …)` schema can name. This module handles it in the only honest way available: **the trailing cohort pass rate is computed over every resolved POC in the window regardless of whether its own account is nameable** (dropping the null-`account_id` rows before computing the cohort rate would compute it over a Won-only, survivorship-biased subset — 91 of 113 Won POCs passed, 80.5%, against the true population rate of 42.5% — which would make the rule almost never fire for the wrong reason); **only the row that would actually be logged requires a real `account_id`**, and that filter is applied after the cohort rate, not before it. The real consequence: the systemic condition (a fail during a low-cohort-rate window) fires **30 times** at `as_of_date` 2025-11-30, but only **1 of those 30** has a traceable account to log. This is a real Phase 1/Phase 2 data gap (account_id on lost opportunities), not a Phase 4 workaround — flagged, not fabricated, the same treatment the marketing-attribution entry gives the identical gap.

### Rule 3 — Post-close committed-Action utilization (`post_close_underutilization`)

**Rule**: fires when a Commercial/Enterprise new-business account's **first full calendar month post-close** (the monthly-grain translation of "30 days post-close") carries committed-Action `utilization_rate < 0.30`. Source: `fact_opportunities` (`account_id`, `close_date`, `segment`, `opportunity_type = 'new_business'`, `is_won = true`) joined to `fact_committed_vs_utilized_monthly` (`account_id`, `month`, `utilization_rate = utilized_actions_monthly / committed_actions_monthly`) on the post-close month. A row with no committed-vs-utilized entry for that month (no commitment on file yet) is excluded, not treated as zero utilization — a missing commitment and a genuinely-unused one are different facts. Action: *"Trigger AM proactive onboarding check-in."*

**Grounding (PROPOSED, not yet confirmed)**: `committed_actions_monthly` is populated **only for Commercial (14,609 of 15,859 account-months) and Enterprise (3,265 of 3,375)** — SMB carries zero, exactly matching the build spec's own company model ("SMB: metered, month-to-month, no contract" — no commitment to have a rate against). Restricting to won new-business Commercial/Enterprise accounts' first full post-close month (432 matched rows at 2025-12-31) gives **median utilization 42.2%, IQR [35.3%, 46.5%]** — the build spec's illustrative 50% floor sits *above* the median and would fire on **85.9%** of newly-closed accounts, which is normal early-ramp behavior, not a risk signal. **30% sits near the 20th–22nd percentile** (10th percentile 23.7%, 25th percentile 35.3%; 22.2% of rows fall below 0.30) — comfortably below the typical ramp pattern, catching a genuine underutilizing minority rather than the ordinary "just closed, not yet fully onboarded" majority.

**A note on what this threshold is not shown to predict**: a same-cohort check of eventual `customer_status` found low-post-close-utilization accounts (< 0.30) churned at a *lower* observed rate (5.2%) than accounts above the floor (13.1%) in this data. Right-censoring was checked and ruled out as the explanation: low-utilization accounts in the matched sample skew *older* by close date (later median close, more elapsed time since close), not more recent, and the churn-rate gap holds within recency quartiles rather than being an artifact of one bucket having had less time to churn. The direction of the gap is real and currently unexplained — stated here rather than used to justify the threshold, and rather than papered over with a plausible-sounding guess. Per the shape decision above, this rule does not need a predictive correlation to be valid — it is a stored, inspectable operational threshold, not a fitted model — but the honest caveat belongs in this doc rather than a silently-omitted footnote.

### Validation — synthetic test cases with known-correct answers

Per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' "Structural/logic artifacts" category: one account that should trigger and one that clearly shouldn't, per rule, run through the exact same `compute_*` functions real-data triggers use (`run_synthetic_scenarios()`). **All 7 scenarios pass** (2 for Rule 1, 2 for Rule 2, 3 for Rule 3 — the third confirms SMB is correctly excluded from Rule 3 even when a synthetic SMB row is given a qualifying low `utilization_rate`, since SMB has no committed-Action concept at all):

| Scenario | What it pins down | Expected |
|---|---|---|
| `ingestion_stall_fires_on_sustained_two_month_dip` | Two consecutive below-threshold months fires once, at the second month | 1 trigger, timestamped at month 2 |
| `ingestion_stall_does_not_fire_on_isolated_single_month` | A single bad month never reaches the persistence bar | 0 triggers |
| `poc_pass_rate_fires_when_own_poc_fails_during_a_low_cohort_rate` | Own POC fails during a genuinely low (1 pass / 5 fail, ~16.7%) trailing cohort | 1 trigger |
| `poc_pass_rate_does_not_fire_when_cohort_rate_is_healthy` | Own POC fails, but the trailing cohort (4 pass / 2 fail, ~66.7%) is healthy — the systemic condition, not the individual failure, gates the rule | 0 triggers |
| `post_close_utilization_fires_below_threshold` | First-month utilization (0.12) below the 0.30 floor | 1 trigger |
| `post_close_utilization_does_not_fire_above_threshold` | First-month utilization (0.55) above the floor | 0 triggers |
| `post_close_utilization_excludes_smb_even_when_below_threshold` | SMB has no committed-Action concept and must never fire this rule regardless of any `utilization_rate` value supplied | 0 triggers |

One synthetic-construction note worth stating: the Rule-2 scenarios place their two cohorts 200+ days apart so the 90-day trailing windows cannot bleed into each other — a construction detail the trailing-window computation depends on, since a synthetic case with its two cohorts inside the same calendar month would produce a false failure despite correct computation logic.

**Real-data run, `as_of_date` 2025-11-30**: **1,558 triggers fired total** — 1,461 ingestion-without-completion, 1 POC-pass-rate (of 30 systemic firings — see Rule 2's account-traceability limitation above), 96 post-close-underutilization. **Second checkpoint, `as_of_date` 2025-06-30**: 1,404 triggers fired (1,319 / 1 / 84), 7 of 7 synthetic scenarios pass — confirming the mechanism is not tuned to one period. Both checkpoints logged to `fact_model_performance_history` (`triggers_fired_total`, `triggers_fired_<rule_id>` per rule, `synthetic_scenarios_passed`/`_total`) via `analytics/model_performance.py`.

### Integration with the weekly executive readout

`analytics/weekly_readout.py`'s "Automated playbook triggers" section calls `analytics/playbook_triggers.py`'s `run_playbook_triggers(as_of_date)` directly and renders "whatever fired this period, unranked (binary, not prioritized)" — build spec Section 5's own phrasing. The readout performs no computation on what it reads, matching every other section's discipline; `outcome` renders as `pending` for every row, since this is a first build with no aged triggers to backtest yet. The readout's own validation suite carries three checks for this section:

- `playbook_triggers_section_is_built_and_present` — the section's status is `present`.
- `playbook_triggers_trace_exactly_to_a_fresh_run` — every rendered trigger row is compared for exact equality against a freshly-run `run_playbook_triggers(as_of_date)`, the same source-trace discipline every other section gets.
- `playbook_triggers_rendered_values_match_structured_payload` — the numeric re-derivation discipline (rebuild the exact expected Markdown line from the structured payload's own fields and require it to appear verbatim), matching the scorecard/watchlist/drill-down checks.

A `playbook_triggers_fired` scalar (1,558 / 1,404 at the two checkpoints) is logged to `fact_model_performance_history` alongside the readout's existing scalars.

### Persistence

`triggers_fired_total`, `triggers_fired_<rule_id>` (one per rule), `synthetic_scenarios_passed`/`_total` are logged to `fact_model_performance_history` via `analytics/model_performance.py`'s `log_performance()`. The full per-trigger detail (every fired row) does not fit that log's flat scalar grain and lives in `fact_playbook_triggers` / `data/playbook_triggers.csv` instead, per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' Persistence note — exactly the artifact this whole build exists to produce, not an omission.

### Target and drift threshold

- **Target**: no accuracy/error target applies (structural/logic artifact, three stored threshold rules, not a fitted model). The synthetic-scenario suite (7/7 pass at build time) stands in its place, per the same convention the variance-diagnostic engine and data-quality-governance entries use.
- **Drift review trigger**: per this doc's general rule, 2 consecutive monthly checkpoints of `synthetic_scenarios_passed` < `synthetic_scenarios_total` → flagged for review (in practice a correctness defect to fix, not a trend, since these are hand-constructed known-answer cases, the same treatment the governance entry gives its own synthetic suite). The genuinely worth-watching series over time is each rule's `triggers_fired_<rule_id>` count: a sudden collapse to near-zero on a rule whose real-data firing count has been stable (this build's own two checkpoints: ingestion 1,319→1,461; POC 1→1; utilization 84→96) would be the leading indicator that an upstream mart or generator change quietly broke that rule's join or its data availability, before anyone notices the CS/SE/AM team stopped receiving that escalation. This is a `drift-monitor` hook, not something this build-time run itself tracks over time.

### Known limitations

- **Rule 2's account-nameable firing rate is severely constrained by a Phase 1/Phase 2 data gap** (`fact_opportunities.account_id` NULL on every lost opportunity): the systemic condition this rule is meant to catch fires 30 times at 2025-11-30, but only 1 of those 30 can be logged against a real account. The rule's logic is correct and validated; its practical usefulness as built is limited until `account_id` is populated on lost opportunities. Flagged, not fabricated — the identical gap `analytics/marketing_attribution.py`'s "Measure caveat" already documents.
- **Rule 3's low-utilization-vs-lower-observed-churn pattern is unresolved.** Right-censoring is checked and ruled out above as the explanation (the low-utilization bucket skews older by close date, not more recent, and the churn-rate gap holds within recency quartiles) — the direction is real and its cause is not yet known, stated as an open question rather than a settled one.
- **All three thresholds are first-build judgment calls**, grounded against this build's own real-data distribution the same way the data-quality-governance entry's PROPOSED floors were, not statistically derived the way the health score's AUC target was. They are handed to `analytics-model-validator` as PROPOSED, not settled.
- **`outcome` is null/pending for every row logged at this build**, by design, per build spec line 118's own stated reason for the log's existence ("needed to eventually backtest whether the triggers are worth keeping, not just to fire them") — no outcome is fabricated to make the log look more complete than a first build actually is. Backfilling `outcome` (e.g., "did the flagged account's completion rate recover within N months," "did the flagged deal eventually close") once triggers have had time to age is future work, most naturally `drift-monitor`'s or a dedicated backtest's, not this build's.
- **No rep-cost/comp or day-grain event data exists anywhere in Phase 1** for any of these three source marts, so "N+ days" is translated to months throughout (stated above) rather than approximated at a finer grain this data cannot actually support.

## Deal-level diagnostics

- **Built**: `analytics/deal_diagnostics.py`. Grain: one row per **open** Commercial/Enterprise new-business opportunity as of `as_of_date`. Reads `fact_opportunities`, `fact_sales_activities`, `fact_opportunity_stage_history`, `fact_forecast_submissions`, `dim_reps` — all `main_marts` per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' read-scope rule. No dbt model was added or changed for this artifact; no Phase 1 generator was touched.
- **What it is**: build spec item #17, Wave 4: "Deal-level diagnostics (the evolution of Pipeline Scanner — per-opportunity risk detection, not just aggregate variance)." A different altitude than `analytics/variance_diagnostic.py`: that engine walks the metric tree at portfolio/aggregate grain; this module answers "which specific live deals look at-risk right now" at per-opportunity grain, using `fact_sales_activities`' individual-touch data the variance engine's mart-of-marts view never sees. It is also distinct from this wave's Automated playbook triggers entry above: Rule 2 there fires on a **cohort-level POC pass-rate threshold** (a systemic condition), logged as an operational event; this artifact scores **every individual open deal**, continuously, and is read live rather than logged as a fired/not-fired event.
- **Scope**: Commercial/Enterprise new-business opportunities only, matching `fact_sales_activities`' own scope exactly (see that mart's grain comment and `generators/sales_activities.py`'s docstring) — SMB has no rep and no activity rows; expansion/renewal are AM-owned and already covered by `am_activity`, not this table.

### Shape decision — hybrid, and explicitly not one type

Following `analytics/forecast.py`'s precedent for this project: a structural/logic half and a genuine classification half, reported side by side rather than fused into one meta-verdict.

| Component | Shape | Validation package |
|---|---|---|
| Six named risk flags (`gone_dark`, `single_threaded`, `competitive_pressure`, `stalled_in_stage`, `forecast_downgraded`, `poc_at_risk`) | Structural/logic. Deterministic threshold rules over already-materialized mart values | Synthetic known-answer test cases (14, all pass), matching `analytics/playbook_triggers.py`'s and `analytics/variance_diagnostic.py`'s pattern. **No coefficient, no AUC, no confusion matrix** |
| Composite risk score (`risk_score` = fitted P(eventual loss)) | Genuine classification model | The full "Classification models" package: model-choice rationale, full coefficient table, held-out AUC, confusion matrix at the real operating threshold, calibration note, sample sizes |

The two are never collapsed into one flag: a deal can carry a high `risk_score` with zero named flags (mechanics look weak but nothing crisp enough to name), or carry a flag (e.g. `poc_at_risk`) while sitting below the score's flagged threshold if every other signal looks healthy. Both are surfaced; a reader decides how to weigh them, the same discipline `analytics/forecast.py`'s four lenses and `analytics/variance_diagnostic.py`'s `mechanism` column both apply to not fusing genuinely different reads into one number.

### The leakage question this artifact had to answer before choosing a shape

This was the single most important design decision, flagged explicitly in the build brief as the most likely failure mode, and it was resolved with an empirical check at build time rather than an a priori assumption in either direction.

**First, the formula-circularity check**: does `generators/opportunities.py` use any signal from `fact_sales_activities` (touch counts, meeting-held rate, etc.) to decide `is_won`? **No** — confirmed by reading the generator pipeline order: `opportunities.py` decides every opportunity's `is_won` from its own drivers (segment, cycle timing, discount, POC, rep ramp) with no dependency on activity data, and `fact_sales_activities` does not exist yet when `opportunities.py` runs (`generators/sales_activities.py` is a later, separate batch that *reads* `opportunities.csv`). There is no formula-circularity of the kind that would make "predict `is_won` from `n_touches`" a literal restatement of how `is_won` was computed.

**Second, the reverse-conditioning check**: `generators/sales_activities.py` reads `is_won` (already decided) to set each opportunity's cadence, held/connect/reply rate and multi-threading probability — "a deal that closed was worked harder and engaged better than one that didn't" (that module's own docstring). This is the identical pattern `generators/opportunities.py` already uses for `poc_outcome` (drawn conditional on the already-decided `is_won`), which `analytics/forecast.py` already accepts as a legitimate, non-leaky feature once gated on reveal — so reverse-conditioning by itself is not disqualifying in this codebase. The open question was whether *this* instance of it is a modest, realistic tilt (like POC outcome, 0.70–0.85-order AUC) or a near-deterministic collapse of the kind the Account health score entry documents finding and had to gate around (0.93–0.99 AUC scoring inside the generator's deterministic pre-churn decline window).

**Checked empirically, not assumed**: a classifier fitted on ENGAGEMENT-ONLY point-in-time features (touches strictly on or before a synthetic evaluation date drawn uniformly across each closed deal's own `[created_date, close_date)`) scored **0.64 AUC at early-lifecycle snapshots** (eval fraction 0.0–0.3 of the deal's life), **0.75 at late-lifecycle snapshots** (0.7–0.99), **0.69–0.72 pooled across the whole lifecycle** — the same order of magnitude as `analytics/forecast.py`'s own deal-mechanics-only ML lens (0.70–0.85 target, 0.8153 achieved), nowhere near the health score's 0.93–0.99 danger zone at any point in a deal's life. This result — not an argument from precedent — is why this module builds a genuine fitted classifier on engagement signals rather than declaring them unusable and falling back to a purely structural shape.

### Inputs and point-in-time design

| Source mart | Columns used | Role |
|---|---|---|
| `fact_opportunities` | `opportunity_id`, `account_id`, `segment`, `opportunity_type`, `rep_id`, `is_won`, `amount`, `poc_outcome`, `created_date`, `close_date` | The open/closed population, deal amount, POC outcome (gated), training label |
| `fact_sales_activities` | `opportunity_id`, `activity_type`, `outcome`, `activity_timestamp`, `contact_ref`, `competitive_signal` | Engagement signals: touch recency/cadence, meeting-held rate, multi-threading breadth, competitive-signal frequency |
| `fact_opportunity_stage_history` | `opportunity_id`, `stage`, `entered_date` | Stage-relative stall, current stage |
| `fact_forecast_submissions` | `opportunity_id`, `snapshot_date`, `rep_forecast_category`, `manager_forecast_category`, `is_manager_downgrade` | Forecast-category trajectory — reuses, at per-deal current-snapshot grain, the Forecast entry's own already-validated rep/manager-gap signal rather than inventing a new one |
| `dim_reps` | `rep_id`, `hire_date` | Rep ramp status |

Every function takes `as_of_date`. Activities filter to `activity_date <= as_of_date`, stage history to `entered_date <= as_of_date` and open stages only, forecast submissions to `snapshot_date <= as_of_date`, opportunities to `created_date <= as_of_date`. `close_date` is used only to decide whether a deal is open (`close_date > as_of_date`) or, for training rows, to bound the synthetic evaluation window — never read as a feature, matching `analytics/forecast.py`'s identical discipline for the same field.

**`discount_rate` and `list_price` are excluded as features, and that finding is reused rather than re-derived.** `analytics/forecast.py` already established that neither is a stable pre-close observation (`generators/forecast.py`: the final negotiated amount and discount are set together at generation time) and that discount carries negligible win/loss signal on closed new-business populations (≤0.35pp gap). Re-deriving that check here would duplicate work already done and documented; it is cited instead.

**Training population and label design.** One row per **closed** Commercial/Enterprise new-business opportunity with `close_date <= as_of_date`, evaluated at one seeded synthetic point-in-time snapshot drawn **uniformly across the deal's own `[created_date, close_date)` span** — not "one call inside the closing quarter" (`analytics/forecast.py`'s approach for its own training rows). This is a deliberate departure, not an oversight: this module's production use is scoring an open deal at an *arbitrary* point in its life, from just-created to nearly-closed, so training has to sample that same range for the two populations to be comparable — the identical comparability discipline `analytics/forecast.py` applies to its own training-vs-production lead-time check, just resolved differently because this artifact's production population is shaped differently. Label = 1 for eventual **loss** (so `risk_score` reads as P(loss), not P(win)). **Seeding**: `_RANDOM_SEED = 42` covers the synthetic evaluation-date draw and both `train_test_split` calls.

**Split — out-of-time, not random**, matching `analytics/forecast.py`'s rationale exactly: sorted by synthetic `eval_date`, last 25% held out, so every holdout row post-dates every training row — the split that matches "fit on closed history, score an open deal now." The stratified random split is computed and reported alongside for comparability, secondary to the headline figure.

### Structural component — six risk flags

Each is a stored threshold rule, grounded against this build's own real-data distribution (the seeded uniform-lifecycle snapshot population described above, n≈1,600–1,700 depending on `as_of_date`), the same discipline `analytics/playbook_triggers.py`'s three rules use — and, per that same discipline, reported honestly including where a flag does **not** show a win-rate differential, not just where it does.

All grounding and win-rate figures below are read directly off `build_training_rows()` / `compute_risk_flags()`'s own output at the canonical checkpoint (`as_of_date` 2025-11-30, n=1,623 seeded lifecycle-snapshot rows) — not a separate approximation, so the numbers here are exactly what the shipped code produces.

| Flag | Rule | Grounding | Win-rate differential (not-flagged → flagged) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `gone_dark` | `days_since_last_touch > 6`, gated on `deal_age_days >= 14` | 6 days sits at the 85th percentile of days-since-last-touch among deals with ≥1 touch and age ≥14 days (n=902); fires on 124 of 1,623 rows (7.6%) | 30.1% → 15.3% (n=124 flagged) — real, modest |
| `single_threaded` | distinct `contact_ref` count ≤ 1, gated on `deal_age_days >= 21` | By day 21 only 11.1% of deals remain single-threaded (vs. 26.7% at a 7-day gate, which would flag over a quarter of all deals and not be a trigger); fires on 74 of 1,623 rows (4.6%) | **30.3% → 0.0%** (n=74 flagged, zero wins) — the strongest single flag in the set |
| `competitive_pressure` | ≥3 `competitive_signal` touches in the trailing 60 days | 3 sits at the 85th percentile of the trailing-60-day competitive-touch-count distribution (n=902); fires on 145 of 1,623 rows (8.9%) | Overall win rate 29.5% → 23.4% (n=145) — real but modest as a general risk signal (elevated on won deals too, per `generators/config.py`'s own `SALES_ACTIVITY_COMPETITIVE_SIGNAL_RATE`: won 0.09, other_loss 0.04). It is a much sharper, specific predictor of *which lost deals lose to a competitor*: among lost deals, fires on 27.0% of `loss_reason == 'competitive'` deals against 0.4–1.1% of every other loss reason (n=389/364/178/222). Read primarily as "competitive risk," not "generic loss risk" |
| `stalled_in_stage` | `days_in_current_stage / (segment's expected cycle days / stage count) > 1.5` | Sits between the 85th (1.12) and 90th (1.33) percentile of the observed ratio (n=1,248 staged rows); fires on 100 of 1,623 rows (6.2%) | 29.1% → 27.0% (n=100) — the smallest differential of the six, consistent with `analytics/forecast.py`'s own near-zero coefficient on the equivalent feature. Kept as a named flag because "stuck in stage far longer than segment norm" is operationally actionable regardless of how weakly it independently predicts outcome here, the same treatment `analytics/playbook_triggers.py`'s Rule 3 gives its own unexplained-direction finding — reported at its real, modest size, not inflated |
| `forecast_downgraded` | rep's latest filed category is Commit/Best Case **and** manager downgraded at that snapshot (`is_manager_downgrade`) | Reuses, at per-deal current-snapshot grain, exactly the Forecast entry's own already-validated signal (there measured as a 20.2pp win-rate penalty over closing-quarter calls specifically); fires on 126 of 1,623 rows (7.8%) | 29.3% → 24.6% (n=126) — same direction as the Forecast entry's finding, more modest because this module's snapshots span a deal's whole lifecycle, not just its closing quarter |
| `poc_at_risk` (Enterprise only) | `poc_outcome == 'fail'`, gated on reveal (`_POC_REVEAL_LAG_DAYS = 14`, identical gate to `analytics/forecast.py`) | Among revealed Enterprise POCs (n=91 of 440 Enterprise new-business rows in the fitted population) | 29.6% → **9.3%** on a revealed fail (n=54); a revealed pass moves it to 37.8% (n=37, base rate 28.8%) — a real but more modest pass-side lift than the fail-side drop, the strongest deal-mechanics flag in the set on the loss side |

All six thresholds are **PROPOSED, not yet confirmed** — first-build judgment calls grounded against this build's own real-data distribution, the same status the playbook-triggers thresholds, the variance engine's ±8%, and the segment-migration 10% floor all carry in this doc.

### Classification component — composite risk score

**Model choice and why — logistic regression, `sklearn`, `random_state=42`, deliberately WITHOUT `class_weight='balanced'`.** Same three reasons `analytics/forecast.py`'s ML lens gives for the identical choice, restated for this artifact's own constraints: (1) the score is read as a risk ranking / watchlist probability, and skipping balancing keeps it honestly calibrated rather than systematically inflated — load-bearing here because a manager reading "71% risk" should be able to read it as roughly what it says; (2) every coefficient is directly attributable to one input, which is what makes the full coefficient table below possible without a secondary explainability technique standing between the model and its own explanation — a deal-risk score a rep is expected to act on has to be auditable; (3) dataset scale (1,352–1,623 closed new-business opportunities in the fitted population depending on `as_of_date`, split 75/25 train/holdout, 24 columns after encoding) is a well-justified fit for a linear model without risking fitting noise. A gradient-boosted alternative was rejected on the same calibration ground `analytics/forecast.py`'s ML lens rejects it for — poor out-of-the-box calibration, requiring a second fitted calibration step between the model and a probability a manager reads directly.

**Features (17 numeric + 2 categorical, 24 columns after encoding)**: engagement — `n_touches`, `n_contacts`, `days_since_last_touch`, `meeting_held_rate`, `touch_rate`, `comp_count_60d`; deal mechanics — `deal_age_days`, `deal_age_ratio`, `has_entered_a_stage`, `stage_progress`, `days_in_current_stage`, `stage_stall_ratio`, `poc_revealed_pass`, `poc_revealed_fail`, `rep_is_ramping`, `rep_tenure_days`; forecast trajectory — `rep_confident_manager_downgrade`; categorical `segment`, `current_stage`. The continuous/underlying versions of the same signals the flags use are fed to the model too (e.g. `comp_count_60d` rather than the flag's own boolean cut) — the flags and the score are two independent reads of overlapping raw signal, not the model consuming its own flags as features.

#### Target — stated before fitting

Recorded per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' rule that an artifact with no stated success criterion can't be validated later. **Pooled out-of-time held-out AUC: 0.60–0.85.** *Floor* below `analytics/forecast.py`'s own 0.70 floor because this module's snapshot population deliberately spans a deal's *entire* lifecycle — mixing near-chance early-life snapshots (0.64 AUC measured in isolation) with much stronger late-life ones (0.75) — where `analytics/forecast.py`'s population is scored only inside the closing quarter. *Ceiling* is the identical leak-alarm figure `analytics/forecast.py` and the Account health score both use for the identical reason: a pooled AUC above 0.85 would be evidence of pattern-matching the generator's `is_won → engagement` construction (see the leakage investigation above) rather than a genuine early-warning signal, and would be investigated as a breach rather than celebrated. **Calibration gap target: |mean predicted probability − actual base rate| ≤ 0.05**, matching `analytics/forecast.py`'s figure and reasoning (the score is read as a probability, not just a rank).

#### Achieved — statistical validation package

**Canonical checkpoint: `as_of_date` 2025-11-30** (43 open Commercial/Enterprise new-business deals — 31 Commercial, 12 Enterprise). **Second checkpoint: `as_of_date` 2025-06-30** (91 open deals — 38 Commercial, 53 Enterprise), confirming the mechanism isn't tuned to one period. `as_of_date = 2025-12-31` is not usable as a checkpoint: zero open new-business opportunities remain in the simulation at that date (the identical end-of-window truncation `analytics/forecast.py`'s and `analytics/variance_diagnostic.py`'s own "Known limitations" sections document).

| Measure | 2025-11-30 | 2025-06-30 | Target | Met (both) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Out-of-time held-out AUC | **0.6965** | **0.7158** | 0.60–0.85 | yes |
| Stratified-random-split AUC (secondary) | 0.7314 | 0.7167 | — | — |
| Calibration gap | **+0.0132** (mean predicted 0.7127 vs. base rate 0.6995) | **−0.0352** (0.6985 vs. 0.7337) | ≤ ±0.05 | yes |
| n_train / n_holdout | 1,217 / 406 | 1,014 / 338 | — | — |
| n_train positive (loss) / n_holdout positive | 869 / 284 | 720 / 248 | — | — |

**Coefficient table** (canonical checkpoint, standardized / one-hot-encoded scale — `StandardScaler` on all 17 numeric inputs, so these compare in relative magnitude, not raw-unit effects; sorted by absolute magnitude; label = 1 for eventual loss, so a positive coefficient raises predicted loss risk):

| Feature | Coefficient |
|---|---|
| deal_age_days | 2.6302 |
| n_touches | −1.4018 |
| n_contacts | −1.2113 |
| current_stage_POC | 0.5309 |
| current_stage_SAL | −0.4975 |
| comp_count_60d | 0.3097 |
| stage_progress | −0.2459 |
| deal_age_ratio | −0.2297 |
| days_since_last_touch | 0.2072 |
| segment_Enterprise | 0.1995 |
| meeting_held_rate | −0.1995 |
| rep_tenure_days | 0.1949 |
| current_stage_Proposal/Negotiation | 0.1918 |
| has_entered_a_stage | 0.1755 |
| stage_stall_ratio | −0.1453 |
| rep_confident_manager_downgrade | 0.1392 |
| days_in_current_stage | 0.1305 |
| poc_revealed_pass | −0.1235 |
| touch_rate | 0.1137 |
| rep_is_ramping | 0.0598 |
| poc_revealed_fail | 0.0566 |
| segment_Commercial | −0.0544 |
| current_stage_pre_stage | −0.0406 |
| current_stage_SQO | −0.0394 |

Three caveats travel with this table, parallel to the ones `analytics/forecast.py`'s own coefficient table carries. (1) `deal_age_days` dominates and is negative-correlated with engagement volume (older deals tend to be the ones that have gone quiet, per `n_touches`/`n_contacts`'s strongly negative coefficients) — the two are related reads of the same underlying "is this deal still moving" signal, not two independent effects of equal weight. (2) `poc_revealed_pass`/`poc_revealed_fail` are structurally zero for every non-Enterprise deal, so their magnitudes describe their effect *within* that stratum. (3) `stage_progress` and the `current_stage` dummies are two representations of one underlying signal, so neither coefficient alone describes the model's reliance on stage position. Note `poc_revealed_fail`'s coefficient (0.0566) reads smaller here than the flag-level grounding above (29.6%→9.3% win rate) would suggest — most of that stratum's effect is being carried jointly with `current_stage_POC` and `segment_Enterprise`, a collinearity effect worth naming rather than reading the standalone coefficient as the whole story.

**Confusion matrix at the production operating threshold** (top quartile of scored probability on the **training** population — matching `analytics/health_score.py`'s quantile-tier precedent for a score whose primary use is a ranking/watchlist, not a fixed 0.5 cutoff): at 2025-11-30, threshold 0.8443, held-out precision **0.8302**, recall 0.3099, F1 0.4513, from TP=88, FP=18, TN=104, FN=196. At 2025-06-30, threshold 0.8455, precision **0.9189**, recall 0.2742, F1 0.4224, from TP=68, FP=6, TN=84, FN=180. The shape is the intended one for a "flagged" watchlist tier: high precision, low-to-moderate recall — a deal the score calls flagged is very likely a genuine eventual loss, and a deal it doesn't flag is not thereby written off (the six named flags and the full continuous `risk_score` remain visible for every open deal regardless of this binary cut).

**Calibration note.** Calibration gap **+0.0132** at 2025-11-30 and **−0.0352** at 2025-06-30, both inside the ±0.05 target — this model **is** calibrated and intended to be read as a probability, no reweighting applied. Reliability by held-out quintile (2025-11-30):

| Predicted-probability bucket | n | Mean predicted | Actual rate |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.006–0.573 | 82 | 0.4053 | 0.4634 |
| 0.573–0.717 | 81 | 0.6519 | 0.6543 |
| 0.717–0.790 | 81 | 0.7581 | 0.7037 |
| 0.790–0.863 | 81 | 0.8281 | 0.8148 |
| 0.863–1.000 | 81 | 0.9237 | 0.8642 |

Monotone and close to the diagonal across all five buckets — no systematic over- or under-confidence at either end.

### Validation — synthetic scenarios (flags) + build-time checks (both components)

`run_synthetic_scenarios()`: 14 hand-constructed cases, two or three per flag (a fires case, a does-not-fire case, and for the two gated flags a boundary case confirming the deal-age gate itself, plus a segment-exclusivity case confirming `poc_at_risk` never fires for Commercial), run through the exact same `compute_risk_flags()` the real diagnostic uses. **14 of 14 pass at build time.**

`run_build_time_validation(as_of_date)` — 9 checks at each checkpoint, **9 of 9 pass at both 2025-11-30 and 2025-06-30**: the synthetic-scenario suite; each of the six flags' selectivity on real open-deal data (non-vacuous, i.e. not literally impossible to fire, reported with its real firing count rather than asserted); `auc_within_target`; `calibration_within_target`.

### Persistence

`auc_holdout_out_of_time`, `auc_holdout_random_split`, `precision_flagged_tier`/`recall_`/`f1_`, the four confusion-matrix cells, `operating_threshold_probability`, `mean_predicted_probability`, `actual_base_rate`, `calibration_gap`, `n_train`, `n_holdout` are logged to `fact_model_performance_history` via `analytics/model_performance.py`'s `log_performance()` under `model_name = 'deal_diagnostics'`. The coefficient table, reliability buckets, and per-flag grounding tables don't fit that log's flat scalar grain and are recorded as structured detail in this entry instead, per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' Persistence note.

### Target and drift threshold

- **Target**: out-of-time held-out AUC 0.60–0.85; calibration gap ≤ ±0.05 (both stated above, before the achieved figures were read into this file). The flag layer has no accuracy target — the synthetic-scenario suite (14/14) and the real-data selectivity checks stand in its place, per the same convention `analytics/playbook_triggers.py`'s and `analytics/variance_diagnostic.py`'s entries use.
- **Drift threshold**: per this doc's general rule, 2 consecutive monthly checkpoints breaching → flagged for review. Concretely: `auc_holdout_out_of_time` outside 0.60–0.85; `calibration_gap` outside ±0.05; `synthetic_flag_scenarios` dropping below 14/14 (a correctness defect to fix, not a trend, the same treatment the playbook-triggers entry gives its own synthetic suite); any flag's real-data firing count collapsing to exactly 0 on a checkpoint where it has previously fired (the leading indicator an upstream mart or generator change quietly broke that flag's join, the same reasoning the playbook-triggers entry gives its own per-rule firing-count series) — a `drift-monitor` hook, not something this build-time run itself tracks over time.

### Known limitations

- **The `stalled_in_stage` flag shows the smallest win-rate differential of the six** (29.1% vs. 27.0%) and is kept for operational actionability rather than demonstrated strong predictive value — stated above, not glossed over, the same treatment `analytics/playbook_triggers.py`'s Rule 3 gives its own unexplained-direction finding.
- **`competitive_pressure` is a competitive-loss-specific signal, not a general loss-risk signal**, and must be read that way — flagged deals are not more likely to lose overall in this data, only more likely, if they do lose, to lose specifically to a named competitor. Presenting it as a generic "risk" flag alongside the other five without this caveat would overstate what it shows.
- **`forecast_downgraded`'s win-rate differential (29.3%→24.6%) is more modest than the Forecast entry's own closing-quarter-scoped measurement of the identical underlying signal (20.2pp)** — attributed to this module's snapshot population spanning a deal's whole lifecycle rather than just its closing quarter, not to a contradiction between the two entries' findings.
- **`account_id` is NULL on the majority of currently-open deals** (35 of 43 at the 2025-11-30 checkpoint) — the identical Phase 1/Phase 2 gap `analytics/playbook_triggers.py`'s Rule 2 and `analytics/marketing_attribution.py`'s "Measure caveat" already document for lost opportunities, evidently present for a deal's eventually-realized outcome even while it is still open in this pre-generated simulation. `account_id` is not read by any flag or by the fitted model (both operate at `opportunity_id` grain) so this does not affect scoring correctness, but it materially limits which flagged deals can be routed to an account owner by account rather than by opportunity ID alone. Flagged, not fabricated, the same treatment the identical gap already receives elsewhere in this doc.
- **No account-grain ARR or dollar-weighted prioritization is applied.** Every open deal's `amount` is available in the scored output but the flags and score treat a $15K Commercial deal and a $750K Enterprise deal identically in their firing logic — a dollar-weighted watchlist ordering (the same caveat the Variance-diagnostic engine's watchlist section documents for its own ARR-at-risk ranking) is a natural next step, not built here.
- **End-of-window truncation**: `as_of_date = 2025-12-31` has zero open new-business opportunities, matching the identical limitation `analytics/forecast.py` and `analytics/variance_diagnostic.py` both document for their own final-month checkpoints.
- **Not yet wired into the weekly executive readout.** `analytics/weekly_readout.py` is out of scope for this build (already-shipped this wave via the Automated playbook triggers build); wiring a deal-level watchlist section into the readout is future work, the same documented-seam treatment the readout gives its own still-deferred narrative-generation piece.

## Rep productivity & coaching diagnostics

- **Built**: `analytics/rep_productivity.py`. Grain: one row per quota-bearing rep (ISR/AE) per trailing-window snapshot — default 4 complete calendar quarters ending at the last complete quarter `<= as_of_date`. Reads `fact_sales_activities`, `fact_opportunities`, `dim_reps` — all `main_marts` per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' read-scope rule. No dbt model was added or changed for this artifact; no Phase 1 generator was touched.
- **What it is**: build spec item #14, Wave 4. Sits between two already-built artifacts and duplicates neither. Capacity planning (item #3) answers "is the team's aggregate quota achievable given its ramp mix" at segment x rep_type x quarter grain — this module never recomputes that productivity/coverage/ramp-mix decomposition; the only thing it borrows is the identical 180-day ramp cutoff and the same "opportunity closed in a complete calendar quarter" attribution rule, both cited from capacity planning rather than re-derived. Automated playbook triggers' three shipped rules (`ingestion_without_completion`, `poc_pass_rate_below_threshold`, `post_close_underutilization`) are all account/opportunity-scoped operational-log triggers, none of them rep-scoped — as of this build there is no cohort-level "underperforming rep" rule in `analytics/playbook_triggers.py` to duplicate. (The build brief that scoped this artifact referred to such a rule as an existing "Rule 3" to avoid re-deriving; that rule does not exist in the shipped module — its three rules are the ones just listed. This is stated here as a build-time finding, not silently corrected.) The generator DOES inject a real, persistent, named underperforming-rep-cohort mechanism (`generators/sales_activities.py`'s `_assign_underperformer_cohort`, `config.SALES_ACTIVITY_UNDERPERFORMER_SHARE = 0.16`), and the QA plan's own test suite (`tests/test_phase1_batch8.py::TestInjectedIncidents::test_underperforming_rep_cohort_is_visible_in_activity_patterns`) already proves that cohort is recoverable from meeting-held rate alone, using only public activity data, never an internal generator flag. This module is what turns that raw recoverability into a real, individual-rep, multi-signal, composition-adjusted diagnostic — named, individual, coaching-actionable, per the build brief's own framing, not a repeat of a cohort-level threshold trigger.
- **Scope — ISR/AE only, deliberately**, matching capacity planning's scope for the identical reason: `fact_sales_activities` itself is scoped to Commercial/Enterprise new-business opportunities (SMB has no rep; AM-owned expansion/renewal activity already lives in `fact_am_activity` and is out of that table's scope by its own header), and only ISR/AE carry a quota (`dim_reps.quota_amount` is null for AM/SE). SE appears in this data only as an occasional Enterprise demo-giver (`config.SALES_ACTIVITY_SE_SHARE_OF_DEMO`), never a deal owner, and is excluded by construction — this module only reads rows whose `rep_id` joins to an ISR/AE row in `dim_reps`, so an SE-attributed demo touch simply never appears.

### The leakage risk this module is built around, and how it's handled

`generators/sales_activities.py`'s own docstring states the mechanism directly: *"is_won tightens cadence and lifts meeting/demo held-rate and call-connect/email-reply rates: a deal that closed was worked harder and engaged better than one that didn't. This reads the opportunity's already-fixed outcome... a historical log generated after the fact."* Confirmed by reading the generator: `_held_rate`, `_connect_rate`, `_reply_rate`, and `_new_contact_probability` all take `is_won` as a direct argument, alongside `rep_is_ramped` and (for cadence and threading specifically) the persistent underperformer flag. A rep whose book happened to close more of its deals therefore shows mechanically HIGHER raw engagement-quality numbers for a reason that has nothing to do with that rep's actual skill — the deal's already-known outcome retroactively inflated the touches logged against it. Naively correlating a rep's raw held/connect/reply rate against raw win rate or attainment would mostly be re-deriving this retrospective-disclosure artifact, not finding a coaching signal — the identical trivial-accuracy failure mode the Account health score entry's calibration note documents for scoring inside a deterministic decline window, and the Capacity planning and Segment migration entries document for a classifier that would mostly re-derive a known generator rule.

A second, separate check was run before choosing a shape: does `generators/opportunities.py` (which decides `is_won`) read anything from `fact_sales_activities`? **No** — `opportunities.py` decides every new-business opportunity's outcome from its own drivers (segment, cycle timing, discount, POC, rep ramp status via `_pick_rep(bias_toward_ramped=...)`) before `fact_sales_activities` exists at all (`generators/sales_activities.py` is a strictly later, separate batch that reads `opportunities.csv`). Critically, rep ASSIGNMENT within a ramp-status bucket is uniform: `_pick_rep`'s selection weights depend only on `ramped` (boolean), never on individual rep identity or the underperformer flag — so which specific ramped rep receives a given won deal is generator-level noise, not a function of anything this module measures. **This means individual-rep win-rate/attainment variance beyond ramp status is not wired to anything in this data** — there is no generator mechanism connecting a rep's activity quality to which deals that rep is assigned. The one real, non-noise, ramp-independent per-rep signal in `fact_sales_activities` is the injected underperformer cohort, and it is wired only into engagement mechanics (cadence, held/connect/reply rate, multi-threading), never into deal assignment or outcome.

**The fix used throughout this module is composition-adjustment, not a raw correlation.** Every rep's actual rate on every metric is compared against a **leave-one-out peer benchmark** computed within the same `(segment, is_won, rep_is_ramped_at_opportunity_creation)` stratum — exactly the three variables the generator conditions each engagement mechanic on. Because both the rep's own actual rate and its peer expectation are computed inside the same strata, a rep whose book happens to have a different won/lost or ramp mix than the pool average is compared only against *other* reps' same-stratum performance, not against the pooled population — the composition effect cancels out of the residual (`actual − expected`) by construction, not by care. This is the module's answer to the leakage question the build brief flagged as the most likely failure mode: it does not avoid the confound by refusing to use `is_won`-conditioned signals at all (which would throw away real information — held rate, connect rate, and multi-threading are genuinely useful engagement-quality signals); it neutralizes the confound structurally, the same way a stratified/Mantel–Haenszel-style adjustment neutralizes a composition effect in any observational comparison.

### Shape decision — structural/descriptive with a composition-adjusted peer benchmark, not a fitted model

Nothing here is fitted: no train/test split, no estimated coefficient, no probability model. Per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' "Structural/logic artifacts" category (the same category capacity planning, segment migration, marketing attribution, data quality governance and playbook triggers all use) — **no coefficient table, no AUC, no confusion matrix, no R²/RMSE, no calibration note applies here, and their absence is deliberate, not pending.**

**Model type selection and rationale (why not a fitted model)**: a propensity-style classifier or regression predicting rep-level win rate or attainment from activity-quality features was the credible alternative, and was rejected on the leakage grounds above — the one real explanatory link a fitted model could rest on (is_won → engagement quality) runs backward (outcome causes the feature, not the reverse), so a model "explaining" attainment from engagement quality would mostly be re-deriving that retrospective-disclosure artifact, exactly the failure mode the leakage section above documents finding and designing around. This is also confirmed empirically, not just theoretically — see the honest-null correlation check below.

### The four coaching-actionable signals, and why touches_per_opp and cadence are one composite, not two

Per rep-window: **touches_per_opp** (intensity), **meeting-held rate**, **call-connect rate**, **email-reply rate**, and **contacts-per-opportunity** (multi-threading breadth — distinct `contact_ref` per opportunity). `mean_inter_touch_gap_days` (cadence) is computed and reported per rep as a corroborating raw statistic but is **not** folded into either composite score: `generators/sales_activities.py`'s own docstring states touches and cadence are "one mechanism, not two independently-tuned ones" (touches are placed by sampling successive gaps until the deal window fills, so a tighter mean gap both fits more touches into the window and *is* the measurable latency signal). Treating both as independent evidence would double-count one underlying signal — the same caveat the Account health score entry states for `login_count_avg_3mo` and `login_frequency_weighted` being "two representations of one underlying login signal."

**Composite construction**: for each of the four metrics above, `actual − expected` (the leave-one-out, composition-adjusted residual) is standardized to a z-score **within the rep's own `rep_type` pool** (AE and ISR are structurally different roles with different base rates and contact-pool sizes — capacity planning's "segment x rep_type is a degenerate cross" finding applies identically here, so pooling z-scores across rep_type would compare apples to oranges). `quality_z` is the mean of however many of {held_rate, connect_rate, reply_rate, contacts_per_opp} clear their own reliability floor (minimum 2 of 4, else NaN → `insufficient_data`). `intensity_z` is `touches_per_opp`'s own z-score alone, never averaged with anything else, for the one-mechanism reason above.

**Reliability floors, adopted not invented**: `held_rate` requires ≥8 resolved meetings — taken directly from `tests/test_phase1_batch8.py`'s own cohort-detection test ("too few reps with enough resolved meetings to detect a cohort" at n<8), reused rather than re-derived for the identical underlying signal. `connect_rate`/`reply_rate` require ≥10 calls/emails (same order of magnitude; calls and emails are far more numerous than meetings per rep at build time, so 10 is a real floor, not a binding one in practice). `touches_per_opp`/`contacts_per_opp` require ≥3 opportunities in the window. A rep-metric below its floor is NaN, not zero, and excluded from its pool's z-score computation entirely — an unreliable cell never silently drags down a composite.

**Flagging — bottom quintile within pool (`_QUANTILE_FLAG_THRESHOLD = 0.20`)**, chosen for the same reason the Account health score's `risk_tier` is quantile-based rather than a fixed z cutoff: residuals are not assumed normal, and a quantile is robust to whatever shape the real distribution takes. **20% is a data-independent choice, not fit to the known injected cohort share** — `analytics/rep_productivity.py` never imports `generators.config` and never reads the generator's internal cohort assignment; the only place `SALES_ACTIVITY_UNDERPERFORMER_SHARE = 0.16` is even mentioned is in this doc, as a validation-time grounding note, matching the same "cite for grounding in prose, never import in production logic" discipline `analytics/capacity_planning.py` already uses for `RAMPING_REP_WIN_ASSIGNMENT_FACTOR`. It is a genuine coincidence, worth stating because it grounds the choice after the fact: an independently-chosen 20% quintile lands close to the true 16% generative share. Selection within the quintile is by **rank** (`nsmallest`), not by comparing every row against a quantile *value* — a value-threshold comparison with `<=` would over-flag whenever several reps share an identical or near-identical residual, a real risk in a pool this size: a deliberately uniform, hand-built peer pool in the synthetic-scenario suite is the check that exercises exactly this case, confirming rank-based selection flags only the intended bottom quintile rather than the whole pool. A pool smaller than 10 reps returns no flags at all rather than a spurious cut (`_MIN_POOL_SIZE_FOR_QUANTILE`); AE (21) and ISR (19) both clear this comfortably at both checkpoints.

**Named diagnostic flags, mutually exclusive and exhaustive, checked by priority order that makes one specific mislabeling structurally impossible** — the same "structurally impossible, not just avoided by care" discipline the build brief asked of the variance-diagnostic engine's Layer-1/Layer-2 identification, applied here to ramp vs. genuine gap:

1. `quality_flag` = bottom quintile of `quality_z` within pool; `intensity_flag` = bottom quintile of `intensity_z` within pool.
2. If either fires: **`volume_and_quality_constrained`** (both), **`engagement_quality_constrained`** (quality only), or **`volume_constrained`** (intensity only) — regardless of ramp status, because both z-scores are already composition-adjusted for ramp status per-opportunity (a predominantly-ramping rep is compared against *other* ramping reps' same-stratum peers, not against ramped peers), so a flag that survives that adjustment cannot be a ramp artifact by construction.
3. Only if neither flag fires: `ramp_majority_share >= 0.50` (opportunity-count-weighted share of the rep's window worked while ramping) → **`ramp_explained_on_par`** — the rep's raw numbers may look low next to the whole population, and that is fully explained by tenure, not a coaching signal. Otherwise → **`on_par`**.
4. If neither composite is computable at all (too few reliable metrics or `n_opps < 3`) → **`insufficient_data`**.

A rep can therefore never receive `ramp_explained_on_par` while also tripping a composition-adjusted threshold — checked directly against real output by `check_ramp_explained_never_coincides_with_a_flag` (see Validation), not just implied by the if/elif order.

**A concrete real-data worked example** (`as_of_date` 2025-12-31, ISR REP-00063): raw meeting-held rate lands this rep in the bottom quintile by simple pooled ranking (0.500, among the lowest 8 of 40 reps) — the rep spent the *entire* trailing window ramping (`ramp_majority_share = 1.0`). Composition-adjusted against other ramping reps specifically, `quality_z = +0.935` — comfortably above par; the raw-looking quality problem is fully explained by tenure and correctly does not flag. The same rep's `intensity_z = −1.226` **does** land in the bottom quintile even after the same ramp-stratum adjustment — a genuine, ramp-independent touch-volume gap that survives the adjustment, and the rep is correctly flagged `volume_constrained`, not `ramp_explained_on_par`. A second example, AE REP-00060 (`ramp_majority_share = 0.9`): both composites land well above par (`quality_z = +2.06`, `intensity_z = +1.30`) → `ramp_explained_on_par` — a ramping rep whose raw numbers might look concerning next to a fully-ramped population is correctly reassured, not flagged.

### Inputs and point-in-time design

| Source mart | Columns used | Role |
|---|---|---|
| `fact_opportunities` | `opportunity_id`, `rep_id`, `segment`, `opportunity_type`, `owner_role`, `is_won`, `amount`, `created_date`, `close_date` | The scored population (Commercial/Enterprise new-business, ISR/AE-owned, `close_date` inside the trailing window); `created_date` is what dates each opportunity's `rep_is_ramped` stratum |
| `fact_sales_activities` | `activity_id`, `rep_id`, `opportunity_id`, `activity_type`, `outcome`, `activity_timestamp`, `contact_ref` | Every touch on an in-window opportunity, aggregated to touches, resolved-meeting held/no_show/etc., call connect, email reply, distinct contacts, inter-touch gap |
| `dim_reps` | `rep_id`, `rep_type`, `hire_date`, `quota_amount`, `period_start_date` | Rep type (pool key), ramp status at opportunity creation, quota for the attainment context |

Every loader takes `as_of_date`: `dim_reps` reads are filtered `period_start_date <= as_of_date` (a period beginning later is future information, matching capacity planning's identical guard), opportunities are filtered `close_date` inside `[window_start, window_end]` **and** `close_date <= as_of_date` (belt-and-suspenders — the window bound alone already implies this, since `window_end <= as_of_date` by construction, but both are checked). Activities are read only for opportunities already admitted to the window by their `close_date` — a touch's own `activity_timestamp` is not separately filtered, since every touch on a deal is already date-bounded to `[created_date, close_date]` by construction in `generators/sales_activities.py`, and the deal's `close_date` is what put it in scope, not the individual touch date. No stochastic step exists in this module — no train/test split, no sampling, no simulation — so no random seed applies, matching `analytics/capacity_planning.py`'s and `analytics/segment_migration.py`'s precedent.

**Window choice, checked against the data, not assumed**: a single calendar quarter was tried first and rejected — median resolved meetings per rep-quarter at build time is ~9, sitting right at this module's own 8-meeting reliability floor, meaning most single-quarter snapshots would fail `held_rate` reliability for a large share of reps. **4 trailing complete quarters** puts every quota-bearing rep with any 2025 activity comfortably above every reliability floor (minimum across all 40 active reps at 2025-12-31: 10 resolved meetings, 34 calls, 25 emails) while staying a genuinely "current" coaching window rather than a whole-career average.

**Coverage limitation, stated rather than hidden**: a rep who closed zero Commercial/Enterprise new-business opportunities inside the trailing window has no row in the opportunity panel at all and is silently absent from the diagnostics table — there is no data to build a panel row from, and a `NaN`-filled row would misrepresent "no data" as "on par." At `as_of_date` 2025-12-31, 40 of 44 quota-bearing reps are scored (4 unscored); at 2025-06-30, 39 of 44 (5 unscored). This is reported here as a known coverage gap, the same discipline capacity planning's `has_opportunity_supply` flag and marketing attribution's censoring bracket both use for their own incomplete-population cases, rather than silently dropped without comment.

### Attainment and win-rate context — reported, not used to derive the flag

`compute_attainment_context()` reports each rep's won ARR ÷ stated quota over the same trailing window, and `compute_rep_metric_table()` reports raw win rate — **the simplest possible per-rep echo of the same two source marts capacity planning already reads, never a re-implementation of capacity planning's coverage-share/ramp-factor/baseline-productivity decomposition.** These are included for context and for the honest-null check below, never as an input to the diagnostic flag itself (the leakage section above is exactly why: attainment is downstream of the same `is_won` that retroactively inflates engagement metrics, so using it to *derive* the flag would reintroduce the confound the composition-adjustment is built to avoid).

**Honest-null finding, checked rather than assumed**: a Mann-Whitney U test comparing attainment between any-flagged reps and `on_par` reps shows **no significant difference at either checkpoint** (2025-12-31: flagged mean 1.278 vs. on-par mean 0.867, p=0.143, n=10 vs. 29; 2025-06-30: flagged mean 0.745 vs. on-par mean 1.006, p=0.306, n=9 vs. 27) — and the **direction of the gap reverses between checkpoints**, which is itself evidence against a real, stable relationship rather than an artifact of one noisy sample. Win rate shows the same pattern (p=0.809 and p=0.207). This is the expected result given the leakage-avoidance reasoning above, not a surprising negative: the generator wires no mechanism connecting the underperformer cohort or activity quality to which deals a rep is assigned or wins (`_pick_rep`'s selection weight depends only on ramp status, never on individual rep identity), so no predictive relationship between this module's flags and attainment/win rate should exist in this data, and none is found. Reported explicitly, the same "stated as an open/settled question, not glossed over" discipline the Automated playbook triggers entry's Rule 3 uses for its own unexplained correlation — except here the direction is explained, not unresolved, because the generator's causal wiring was checked directly rather than inferred from the correlation alone.

### Validation — synthetic known-answer cases + real-data non-vacuousness, not statistics

Per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' "Structural/logic artifacts" category: no coefficient, no AUC, no confusion matrix, no R²/RMSE. Two validation layers, both in `run_build_time_validation()`.

**`run_synthetic_scenarios()`** — a hand-built population (10 baseline ramped AE peers with identical behavior, 3 baseline ramping AE peers, and 5 named test reps, every rep clearing every reliability floor on its own) run through the exact same `compute_rep_diagnostics()` pipeline real data uses:

| Scenario | Engineered pattern | Expected verdict |
|---|---|---|
| `syn_volume_low` | Quality matches peers exactly; touches_per_opp roughly halved | `volume_constrained` |
| `syn_quality_low` | touches_per_opp matches peers exactly; held/connect/reply/contacts all well below peer rate | `engagement_quality_constrained` |
| `syn_both_low` | Both volume and quality below peers | `volume_and_quality_constrained` |
| `syn_ramp_explained` | Entirely ramping-stratum opportunities; raw numbers well below the ramped population but at/above the ramping-stratum peer benchmark once adjusted | `ramp_explained_on_par` |
| `syn_on_par` | Matches ramped peer rates exactly on every dimension | `on_par` |

**5 of 5 pass** at build time. (One real bug was caught and fixed during this construction, not merely avoided: the first version of `_quantile_flag` compared every row against a quantile *value* with `<=`, which over-flagged the entire tied baseline-peer pool whenever several reps shared an identical residual — exactly what a deliberately-uniform synthetic peer pool does, and a real possibility in smaller real-data pools too. Fixed to select the bottom-N by rank instead; see the flag-construction section above.)

**Structural checks**, run against a fresh real-data diagnostics run at each checkpoint:

| Check | What it pins down | Result (2025-12-31 / 2025-06-30) |
|---|---|---|
| `flags_are_mutually_exclusive_and_exhaustive` | Every scored rep gets exactly one of the six named verdicts | Pass / Pass (0 bad rows both) |
| `ramp_explained_never_coincides_with_a_flag` | No rep carries `ramp_explained_on_par` while `quality_flag` or `intensity_flag` is also True — the priority-order guarantee, checked directly against output rather than just implied by the code | Pass / Pass (0 bad rows both) |
| `cohort_recovery_against_raw_quantile` | Non-vacuousness, in the spirit of capacity planning's `ramp_mechanism_is_real_not_vacuous`: the composition-adjusted `engagement_quality`-flagged set should be materially enriched among the bottom-quintile-by-**raw** held rate. This reuses the same *method* (raw held-rate quantile, on public activity data) `tests/test_phase1_batch8.py`'s injected-cohort test already proves recovers a real signal — it is not the same check: that test runs quantile 0.16 with a value-based cutoff over the full un-windowed history (43 reps, all-time), while this one runs quantile 0.20 with a rank-based cutoff over this module's own `as_of_date`-windowed population (40/39 reps), comparing against its own composition-adjusted flag rather than the raw quantile alone. Bar: ≥50% overlap (a real minority of legitimate disagreement is expected and is the artifact's value-add, not a failure) | **87.5%** overlap (7/8) at 2025-12-31 / **75%** overlap (6/8) at 2025-06-30 — both pass |

**3 of 3 structural checks pass at both checkpoints.** Sample sizes: 40 reps scored at 2025-12-31 (AE 21, ISR 19), 39 at 2025-06-30 (AE 21, ISR 18).

### Persistence

`synthetic_scenarios_passed`/`_total`, `structural_checks_passed`/`_total`, `reps_scored`, and `reps_flagged_<flag_name>` (one per verdict) are logged to `fact_model_performance_history` via `analytics/model_performance.py`'s `log_performance()` under `model_name = 'rep_productivity'`. The per-rep metric table, coefficient-free composite scores, and the worked examples above don't fit that log's flat scalar grain and are recorded as structured detail in this entry instead, per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' Persistence note.

### Target and drift threshold

- **Target**: no accuracy/error target applies (structural/descriptive, composition-adjusted peer benchmark, not a fitted model). The synthetic-scenario suite (5/5) and the three structural checks (3/3, including the ≥50% real-data cohort-recovery bar — **PROPOSED, not yet confirmed**, grounded above against this build's own real-data run rather than derived from first principles) stand in its place, per the same convention the variance-diagnostic engine, capacity planning, marketing attribution, data quality governance, playbook triggers and deal-level diagnostics entries all use for their own structural components.
- **Drift review trigger**: per this doc's general rule, 2 consecutive monthly checkpoints below either bar → flagged for review. `synthetic_scenarios_passed` dropping below 5/5 is a correctness defect to fix, not a trend (hand-constructed known-answer cases, the same treatment the playbook-triggers and deal-diagnostics entries give their own synthetic suites). `cohort_recovery_against_raw_quantile`'s enrichment rate is the genuinely worth-watching series: a collapse toward 0% would mean the composition-adjustment and the raw ranking have stopped agreeing on who looks bad at all — either a real change in the underlying data or a defect in the stratification join — a `drift-monitor` hook, not something this build-time run itself tracks over time. `reps_flagged_<flag_name>` collapsing to exactly 0 on a flag that has previously fired is the same leading-indicator pattern the playbook-triggers and deal-diagnostics entries already use for their own per-rule/per-flag firing counts.

### Known limitations

- **The ≥50% cohort-recovery bar is a first-build judgment call**, grounded against this build's own two checkpoints (87.5% and 75%) rather than derived from a larger reference distribution — handed to `analytics-model-validator` as PROPOSED, the same status every other first-build threshold in this doc carries.
- **Real-world predictive validity is untestable in this dataset, by construction, not by omission.** The honest-null result above (no significant, direction-stable relationship to attainment/win-rate) is expected precisely because individual-rep skill isn't wired into outcomes anywhere in this generator — rep assignment within a ramp bucket is uniform-random. A correlation here would be more suspicious than reassuring; its absence is not evidence against the diagnostic, but it also means this artifact's practical coaching value can't be demonstrated against this data and would need a real deployment (or a generator change wiring in genuine per-rep skill variance) to test.
- **`ramp_majority_share` is opportunity-count-weighted, not touch-count-weighted.** A rep with one large-touch-volume ramped opportunity and two small-touch-volume ramping ones would be classified "predominantly ramping" by count even though most of their actual touches happened while ramped. This mirrors the "opportunity is the atomic unit of a rep's book" framing this module's attainment and win-rate figures already use, rather than an inconsistent third weighting scheme, but is a real simplification worth naming.
- **Coverage gap**: 4–5 of 44 quota-bearing reps are unscored per checkpoint (zero closed new-business opportunities in the trailing window) — stated above, not hidden. A rep who is entirely inactive on new-business (e.g., a departed rep whose book was reassigned, or a rep who spent the whole window on renewals/expansion before a role change) genuinely cannot be diagnosed for *this* window from this data; extending the trailing window would recover some of them at the cost of recency.
- **No dollar-weighting.** Every rep's flag treats their book uniformly regardless of deal size — a rep working a handful of large Enterprise deals and a rep working many small Commercial deals get the same flag-construction treatment (composition-adjustment for segment already partially addresses this, since segment is one of the three stratification variables, but no ARR-at-risk-style weighting is applied within a rep's own book). The Deal-level diagnostics entry's own "Known limitations" section documents the identical caveat for its per-deal flags.
- **No rep-cost/comp data exists anywhere in Phase 1**, so this module — like Capacity planning — cannot connect a coaching flag to a cost-of-underperformance figure. Same root gap the Capacity planning entry already documents for the "Rep fully-loaded cost, incl. ramp" metric-tree leaf; not re-documented in full here.

## Pricing / packaging analytics

- **Built**: `analytics/pricing_packaging.py`. Two grains: (a) discount realization, discount-vs-outcome and deal-size trend are one row per opportunity at close (source: `fact_opportunities`); (b) packaging/committed-usage calibration is one row per account per month (source: `fact_committed_vs_utilized_monthly`). No dbt model was added or changed for this artifact, and no Phase 1 generator or raw table was added — `list_price`, `discount_rate` and the committed-vs-utilized mart all already exist as real generated data.
- **What it is**: build spec item #16, Wave 5. Unlike its Wave-5 siblings (Territory/coverage & routing, TAM/ICP), this artifact does not run off `market_universe` at all — it is fully served by `fact_opportunities` and `fact_committed_vs_utilized_monthly`, so it was built without waiting on the concurrent territory-dimension extension or reaching into `dim_reps`/`dim_market_universe`/`mart_tam_whitespace`/`analytics/tam_icp.py`, all of which this build deliberately did not touch.
- **Relationship to the metric tree's "Discount rate vs. list" and "Deal-size trend within segment band" leaves, and to the separate `variance_diagnostic.py` fix**: `acme-corp-gtm-metric-tree.md`'s New Logo branch names "Avg initial commitment" (Layer 2: "Realized deal size at close, by segment") with two Layer-3 leaves — "Discount rate vs. list" and "Deal-size trend within segment band." A separate, already-flagged piece of work (not part of this artifact, tracked separately) corrects `analytics/variance_diagnostic.py`'s stale `NOT_COMPUTABLE` marking on those two leaves plus `loss_reason_mix`, now that `fact_opportunities` is confirmed to expose `list_price`/`discount_rate`/`loss_reason`. **This artifact is not that fix and does not modify `variance_diagnostic.py`.** That fix makes two tree leaves compute a single ratio each for the weekly readout's drill-down; this module is the deeper, standalone Phase 4 diagnostic the build spec's "Pricing / packaging analytics" item actually calls for — discount-vs-outcome relationships (win rate, competitive pressure), packaging/committed-usage calibration, and the deal-size-trend read over time, not a single-leaf tie-out. `compute_discount_realization()` computes the same underlying ratio (`1 - amount/list_price`) that leaf will read once the fix lands, and the two are expected to agree by construction — confirmed directly by `check_discount_reconciliation()` below (max abs diff 6.46e-05, i.e. rounding noise only).
- **Shape decision — structural/descriptive, with one seeded non-parametric test in place of a fitted model**: discount realization, packaging calibration and deal-size trend are all direct, deterministic aggregations of already-materialized mart rows. Per `analytics-engineering-conventions`' **"Structural/logic artifacts"** category — the same reasoning the Segment migration, Variance-diagnostic engine, Capacity planning, Marketing attribution, Data quality governance and Playbook triggers entries all use — **there is no coefficient table, no R²/RMSE, no residual diagnostics, no AUC, no confusion matrix and no calibration note here, and their absence is deliberate, not pending.** The one place this module does more than aggregate is the discount-vs-win-rate and discount-vs-competitive-pressure checks, which need to distinguish a real relationship from sampling noise; rather than introduce `scipy` (unused anywhere else in `analytics/`) for a parametric t-test, both use a **seeded permutation test** on the mean-discount difference (label-shuffle null, `numpy`-only, `_RANDOM_SEED = 42` matching the health score's convention) — the more honest tool here since `discount_rate` is uniform-by-segment at generation, not normally distributed.
- **Model type selection and rationale (why not a fitted win-probability-uplift-from-discount model)**: a logistic regression of `is_won` on `discount_rate` (plus controls) was the credible predictive alternative and was rejected on the same class of grounds the Segment migration and Capacity planning entries reject a fitted model, but with the **inverted** data-generating story. `generators/opportunities.py`'s `_sample_discount_and_list_price()` draws `discount_rate` from `rng.uniform(*config.DISCOUNT_RATE_RANGE[segment])` **independently of `is_won`, `loss_reason` and `amount`** — the identical draw runs on both the won and lost branches of `generate_new_business_opportunities()`, with no shared randomness and no causal wire between them (verified by reading the generator, not assumed). There is therefore no real relationship in this data for a classifier to fit; a logistic regression would report a near-zero, statistically insignificant coefficient dressed up as a fitted model — worse than reporting the same null finding directly as what it is, a population-level, checkable absence of a relationship. This is confirmed empirically below, not just argued from the generator: the permutation tests find no significant discount-outcome relationship, while a positive-control check (`forecast_category` vs. `is_won`, a deliberately strong, known-real driver the generator does wire to outcome) shows the test methodology has real power to detect a relationship when one exists. A genuinely predictive deal-level model (e.g. "will this specific open deal need a deep discount to close") is a different question and already has its own home — build spec item #17, Deal-level diagnostics (`analytics/deal_diagnostics.py`, Wave 4) — and is not duplicated here.
- **Inputs**, both read from dbt's marts layer:
  - `fact_opportunities` (`opportunity_id`, `segment`, `opportunity_type`, `is_won`, `loss_reason`, `forecast_category`, `amount`, `list_price`, `discount_rate`, `created_date`, `close_date`) — every row is already closed (no open-pipeline rows exist in this mart), so `close_date <= as_of_date` is the complete point-in-time guard needed.
